It is not hard to convince oneself that for all
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}\}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and for all
j_{1},j_{2},\ldots j_{r}\in\{1,2,\ldots n\}

such that
j_{\ell}\neq j_{\ell+1}
we have:

P_{j_{1}}(A_{i_{1}})P_{j_{2}}(A_{i_{2}})\cdots P_{j_{k}}(A_{i_{k}})[\Omega]=e_{i_{1}}^{\otimes j_{1}}e_{i_{2}}^{\otimes j_{2}}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{\otimes j_{k}}

(7.20)

In other words, we have an embedding of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}\subseteq B(\mathfrak{F})
into
\mathfrak{F}
which is given by:

X\mapsto X[\Omega]

(7.21)

Topological considerations

Let us remark that there is no natural inner product on
B(\mathfrak{F})
. Nevertheless, the embedding of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
into
\mathfrak{F}
allows us to
induce
an inner product on
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
.
Even with this induced inner product, the vector space
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
is
not
a Hilbert space, since it is
not
closed under the topology induced by the inner product.

7.4
Main result

7.4.1
Statement

For each
i=1,2,\ldots n
, let
\Poly(A_{i})
denote the subalgebra of
B(\mathfrak{F})
generated by
A_{i}
and let
\alg(A_{i})
denote the closure of
\Poly(A_{i})
in the weak operator topology of
B(\mathfrak{F})
.

Similarly let

\comm(A_{i})
the sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
consisting of all elements which commute with
A_{i}
. One can show that
\comm(A_{i})
is already closed under the weak operator topology.

The goal of the next two sections is to prove the following result:

Theorem 7.4.1
.

\boxed{\alg(A_{i})=\comm(A_{i})}

(7.22)

As a consequence of Theorem
7.4.1
we have:

Theorem 7.4.2
.

For
n\geq 2
the center of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
is trivial.

Proof.

Suppose that
x
lies in the center of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
. It follows that
x
commutes with
A_{1}
, and
x
commutes with
A_{2}
. Now, by Theorem
7.4.1
we have
x\in\alg(A_{1})
and
x\in\alg(A_{2})
.
But:

\alg(A_{i})\cap\alg(A_{j})=\mathbb{C}.1

The result follows.
∎

Note that in the language of Von Neumann algebras Theorem
7.4.2
implies that
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
is a
factor
. This result is already known
[
38
]
. Our proof is still interesting however as it yields a number of explicit projection formulae (see section
7.4.5
).

7.4.2
Topological considerations

We shall use the notation
\mathbb{A}_{n}
to denote the closure of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
under the natural topology of
\mathfrak{F}
.
Furthermore we shall use the notation
\mathbb{A}_{n}^{d}
to denote the intersection of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
with the finite dimensional subspace of
\mathfrak{F}
consisting of elements of degree
d
.

Lemma 7.4.1
.

For any vector subspace
V
of some Hilbert space
H
, the orthogonal complement
V^{\perp}
is closed.

Let
\overline{\alg(A_{i})}
denote the closure of
\alg(A_{i})
with respect to the topology of
\mathfrak{F}
.

Lemma 7.4.2
.

\overline{\alg(A_{i})}\cap\mathfrak{A}_{n}=\alg(A_{i})

(7.23)

Proof.

Let
V_{i}
denote the vector space spanned by
\{e_{i}^{\otimes n}\}_{n\geq 0}
.
Under the embedding of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
into
\mathfrak{F}
we have:

\Poly(A_{i})=V_{i}

(7.24)

Let
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
denote the closure of
V_{i}
under the topology of
\mathfrak{F}
. We have

\overline{\alg(A_{i})}=\mathfrak{h}_{i}

(7.25)

But we also have that

\mathfrak{A}_{n}\cap\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\alg(A_{i})\cap\mathfrak{h}_{i}
.
Referring back to remarks made in section
7.2
, every bounded measurable function is also square integrable, that is to say:

\alg(A_{i})\subset\mathfrak{h}_{i}

and so:

\alg(A_{i})\cap\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\alg(A_{i})

The result follows.
∎

Let
\overline{\comm(A_{i})}
denote the closure of
\comm(A_{i})
with respect to the topology of
\mathfrak{F}
.

Lemma 7.4.3
.

\overline{\comm(A_{i})}\cap\mathfrak{A}_{n}=\comm(A_{i})

(7.26)

Proof.

We have:

\displaystyle\overline{\comm(A_{i})}

\displaystyle=\overline{\{x\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}\,|\,xA_{i}=A_{i}x\}}

(7.27)

and so:

\displaystyle\overline{\comm(A_{i})}\cap\mathfrak{A}_{n}

\displaystyle=\overline{\{x\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}\,|\,xA_{i}=A_{i}x\}}\cap\mathfrak{A}_{n}

(7.29)

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}\,|\,xA_{i}=A_{i}x\}

(7.30)

\displaystyle=\comm(A_{i})

(7.31)

The result follows.
∎

Strategy

We shall begin by proving the following:

Proposition 7.4.1
.

\boxed{\comm(A_{i})^{\perp}=\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}}

(7.32)

This implies in particular that:

\overline{\alg(A_{i})}=\overline{\comm(A_{i})}

(7.33)

Taking the intersection of both sides with
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
we obtain Theorem
7.4.1

Our goal is now to prove proposition
7.4.1
. It is clear that we have:

\comm(A_{i})^{\perp}\subseteq\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}

(7.34)

We need only to prove the reverse inclusion of equation
7.34
.

\boxed{\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}\subseteq\comm(A_{i})^{\perp}}

(7.35)

7.4.3
Commutators

Recall that the
commutator
of two operators
X
and
Y
is given by:

[X,Y]=XY-YX

(7.36)

Lemma 7.4.4
.

For all
x,y\in\mathfrak{A}
we have:

\tau(x[A_{i},y])=\tau(y[x,A_{i}])

(7.37)

Proof.

By the linearity and the traciality of the state
\tau
, we have:

\displaystyle\tau(x[A_{i},y])

\displaystyle=\tau(xA_{i}y)-\tau(xyA_{i})

\displaystyle=\tau(yxA_{i})-\tau(yA_{i}x)

\displaystyle=\tau(y[x,A_{i}])

∎

Our plan is to study the following family of finite dimensional vector subspaces:

\boxed{\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}_{n}^{d}\}}

(7.38)

Lemma 7.4.5
.

For all
d\geq 1
we have the following inclusion:

\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}_{n}^{d}\}\subseteq\comm(A_{i})^{\perp}

(7.39)

Proof.

Choose any
y\in\mathbb{A}_{n}^{d}
. By lemma
7.4.4
we have that if
x\in\comm(A_{i})
then:

\tau(x[A_{i},y])=\tau(y[x,A_{i}])=0

In other words
[A_{i},y]
lies in the orthogonal complement of
\comm(A_{i})
.
∎

Lemma 7.4.6
.

For all
d\geq 1

\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}\not\subseteq\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}_{n}^{d}\}

Proof.

For any
j\neq i
we have
A_{j}\in\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}
but we do not have
A_{j}\in\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}_{n}^{d}\}
for any
d
.
∎

Our goal in the next section will be to show that:

Proposition 7.4.2
.

\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}\subseteq\overline{\bigcup_{d\geq 1}\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}^{d}_{n}\}}

(7.40)

That is to say, for any
B\in\alg(A_{i})^{\perp}
there exists a sequence of operators
\{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\}

with:
y_{m}\in\mathbb{A}^{d}_{n}
for some
d
which depends on
m
.
such that, as a vector:

B=\lim_{m\to\infty}[A_{i},y_{m}]

(7.41)

Note that this is to be understood as convergence in the metric space induced by the inner product of the Fock space
\mathfrak{F}
.

Proposition
7.4.2
and Lemma
7.4.5
will together give us equation
7.35
, thus proving Theorem
7.4.1
.

7.4.4
Projection formula

In this section we prove proposition
7.4.2
for
i=1
. The general result follows by symmetry.

Notation

Let
S
be any non-trivial element of
\mathfrak{A}
of the form:

S=P_{j_{1}}(A_{i_{1}})P_{j_{2}}(A_{i_{2}})\cdots P_{j_{r}}(A_{i_{r}})

such that
j_{1},j_{2},\ldots j_{r}\geq 1
and
j_{\ell}\neq j_{\ell+1}
as well as
i_{1}\neq 1\neq i_{r}
.
We have:

S\in\alg(A_{1})^{\perp}

Next, for each
n,k\geq 1
let:

S_{n,k}=P_{n}(A_{1})SP_{k}(A_{1})

Again we have:

S_{n,k}\in\alg(A_{1})^{\perp}

In fact, the collection of all operators of the form
S_{n,k}
for all possible choices of
S
form a Hilbert basis for
\alg(A_{1})^{\perp}

Simplification

Lemma 7.4.7
.

If
a
and
c
both commute with
x
then:

[x,abc]=a[x,b]c

Proof.

\displaystyle[x,abc]

\displaystyle=xabc-abcx

\displaystyle=axbc-abxc

\displaystyle=a[x,b]c

∎

Proposition 7.4.3
.

If:

S\in\overline{\bigcup_{d\geq 1}\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}^{d}_{n}\}}

Then, for all
n,k\geq 0
we have:

S_{n,k}\in\overline{\bigcup_{d\geq 1}\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}^{d}_{n}\}}

Proof.

Suppose that we already have a sequence of operators
\{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\}
with:

y_{m}\in\mathbb{A}_{n}

such that:

S=\lim_{m\to\infty}[A_{1},y_{m}]

It follows that:

\displaystyle S_{n,k}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}P_{n}(A_{1})[A_{1},y_{m}]P_{k}(A_{1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}[A_{1},P_{n}(A_{1})\,y_{m}\,P_{k}(A_{1})]

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}[A_{1},z_{m}]

where
z_{m}=P_{n}(A_{1})\,y_{m}\,P_{k}(A_{1})
. The result follows
∎

Projection formula for arbitrary
S
:

\boxed{S=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{2m+3}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(m-k)([S_{k,k+1},A_{1}]-[S_{k+1,k},A_{1}])}

(7.42)

In order to prove this formula we will need some more notation.
For fixed
S
, let
W
denote the vector subspace spanned by all vectors of the form:

\displaystyle v_{n,k}

\displaystyle=[S_{n,k},A_{1}]

\displaystyle=S_{n,k+1}-S_{n+1,k}+S_{n,k-1}-S_{n-1,k}

The second equality above is a consequence of the three-term recurrence for Chebyshev polynomials (see equation
7.6
).
It will be convenient to make the following change of notation:

\displaystyle e_{n,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(S_{n,k}+S_{k,n})\mbox{ for }n<k

\displaystyle e_{k,k}

\displaystyle=S_{k,k}

\displaystyle w_{n,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(v_{n,k}-v_{k,n})\mbox{ for }n<k

We may now rewrite equation
7.42
as follows:

\displaystyle s_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m+3}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(m-k)([S_{k,k+1},A_{1}]-[S_{k+1,k},A_{1}])

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2m+3}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(m-k)w_{k,k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m+3}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(m-k)(\sqrt{2}e_{k,k+2}-2e_{k+1,k+1}+2e_{k,k}-\sqrt{2}e_{k-1,k+1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m+3}\left(2me_{0,0}+\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}e_{k,k+2}-2\sum_{k=1}^{m}e_{k,k}\right)

Suppose that
S
is an element of degree
t=j_{1}+j_{2}+\cdots+j_{r}
. For each
m\geq 0
let us define:

E_{S}^{2m}\subset\bigcup_{d=0}^{m}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2d+t}

(7.43)

to be the vector space spanned by the
e_{n,k}
for
n+k
even and
n+k\leq 2m
.
Similarly, let us define, for each
m\geq 0
the vector subspace:

W_{S}^{2m-1}\subset E_{S}^{2m}

(7.44)

to be the vector space spanned by the
w_{n,k}
for
n+k
odd,
n<k
and
n+k\leq 2m-1
.

Proposition 7.4.4
.

The vector
s_{m}
is the orthogonal projection of the vector
S
onto the finite dimensional subspace
W_{S}^{2m-1}
.

Proof.

We shall show that the vector
(S-s_{m})
lies in the orthogonal complement of
W_{S}^{2m-1}
with respect to
E_{S}^{2m}
.
We begin by observing that if
r>2
then:

\langle S,e_{k,k+r}\rangle=\langle s_{m},e_{k,k+r}\rangle=0

Thus we may restrict ourselves even further to the subspace:

F\subseteq E_{S}^{2m}

(7.45)

spanned by vectors of the form
e_{k,k}
or
e_{k,k+2}
with degree less than or equal to
2m
.
Next, if
j-i\geq 4
then:

\langle e_{k,k},w_{i,j}\rangle=\langle e_{k,k+2},w_{i,j}\rangle=0

Let
U
denote the vector subspace of
W_{S}^{2m-1}
spanned by vectors of the form
w_{k,k+1}
for
k<m

and
w_{k,k+3}
for
k<m-1
. We must show that for all
u\in U
we have:

\langle S-S_{m}|u\rangle=0

Now:

(2m+3)\langle S|w_{0,1}\rangle=\langle e_{0,0}|\sqrt{2}e_{0,0}\rangle=-(2m+3)\sqrt{2}

While:

\displaystyle(2m+3)\langle s_{m}|w_{0,1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 2me_{0,0}|\sqrt{2}e_{0,0}\rangle+\langle\sqrt{2}e_{0,2},e_{0,2}\rangle+\langle-2e_{1,1}|-\sqrt{2}e_{1,1}\rangle

\displaystyle=(2m+3)\sqrt{2}

Next, for all
i>0
we have:

\langle S|w_{i,i+1}\rangle=0

and:

\displaystyle(2m+3)\langle s_{m}|w_{i,i+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sqrt{2}e_{i,i+2},e_{i,i+2}\rangle-\langle 2e_{i,i},\sqrt{2}e_{i,i}\rangle

\displaystyle\phantom{====}+\langle 2e_{i+1,i+1},\sqrt{2}e_{i+1,i+1}\rangle-\langle\sqrt{2}e_{i-1,i+1}|e_{i+1,i+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}-2\sqrt{2}+2\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{2}

\displaystyle=0

Finally, for all
i\geq 0
we have:

\langle S|w_{i,i+3}\rangle=0

While:

\displaystyle(2m+3)\langle s_{m}|w_{i,i+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sqrt{2}e_{i,i+2},e_{i,i+2}\rangle-\langle 2e_{i,i},\sqrt{2}e_{i,i}\rangle

\displaystyle\phantom{====}+\langle 2e_{i+1,i+1},\sqrt{2}e_{i+1,i+1}\rangle-\langle\sqrt{2}e_{i-1,i+1}|e_{i+1,i+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}-2\sqrt{2}+2\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{2}

\displaystyle=0

The result follows.

∎

It remains to verify that:

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}||s_{m}||

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2m+3)^{2}}\left(4m^{2}+2m+4m\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{2m}{2m+3}

\displaystyle=1

We have proven equation
7.42
and shown that:

S\in\overline{\bigcup_{d\geq 1}\{[A_{i},y],y\in\mathbb{A}^{d}_{n}\}}

Theorems
7.4.1
and
7.4.2
follow as a consequence of proposition
7.4.3
.

7.4.5
An interesting matrix

Let us fix the following order on the wet of vectors
w_{n,k}
for
n+k
odd and
n<k
and
n+k\leq 2m-1
:

For each
m\geq 1
let
A_{m}
denote the
Gramm matrix
of inner products
\langle w_{n,k},w_{n^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle
for
n+k
odd and
n+k\leq 2m-1
.

B_{m}=(2m+3)A_{m}^{-1}
. Here is the matrix

We have used some colour to highlight the self-similar structure of this matrix.

We may block diagonalize the matrix
B_{5}
by conjugating by the following matrix:

U=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|cc|ccc|cccc|ccccc}1&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0&\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0\\

Note that:

{\color[rgb]{0,1,0.5}13}={\color[rgb]{0,1,0}7}+{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}18}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}

These examples suggest that there is some rich underlying structure which is worth further pursuit.

7.5
Conclusion

We have given a elementary proof that semicircular algebra is a factor in the sense of Von Neumann algebras. Although this result is already known, our proof is particularly simple. We have also given an explicit formula for the projection of any element onto the dense subspace of commutators.
It would be interesting to see if this approach could be extended to the case of the
q
-semicircular algebra
[
4
]
, or even the
(q,t)
-semicircular algebra
[
2
]
.

Examining the Gramm matrix of the most natural basis for the space of commutators, we have found it to have an intriguing self-similar structure which may be interesting to investigate further.
